III.2
Experimental signatures of chirality

Very generally, the enantiomorphism realized in unichiral and
multichiral media can be identified via observable effects such as
natural optical activity
[
51
]
and nonreciprocal directional
dichroism
[
52
]
that have opposite signs for the different
enantiomorphs. The occurrence of these effects is not limited to
chiral media, i.e., their observability represents a necessary but
not a sufficient criterion for enantiomorphism
[
13
,
45
,
133
]
. However, in nonenantiomorphous media, these effects arise
such that symmetry-equivalent directions of the media show the same
effect with opposite signs
[
13
,
133
]
. If this case can be
excluded experimentally, i.e., opposite signs are observed only for
different enantiomorphs, a phenomenon like natural optical activity
becomes a necessary and sufficient criterion for enantiomorphism.

This aspect of enantiomorphs clearly distinguishes, e.g.,
(anti)magnetochiral domains from ordinary magnetic domains. It also
allows one to differentiate between opposite enantiomorphs.
Multichiral systems require multiple such probes to distinguish all
four enantiomorphs. In the following, we focus on optical
nonreciprocities.

III.2.1
Optical nonreciprocity: General introduction

The reduced symmetry of chiral systems generally allows additional
contributions to the dielectric tensor that give rise to distinctive
optical effects
[
51
]
. To illustrate most clearly the dual
and triadic relationships between unichiral systems, in the
following, we focus on the phenomenon of
nonreciprocal
directional dichroism
(NDD), i.e., the different rates of
absorption for unpolarized light beams propagating in opposite
directions. Fundamentally, NDD occurs in materials allowing a
third-rank Cartesian tensor with signature
--

[
52
]
. A
subset of these materials are crystals permitting a magnetotoroidal
vector
T_{1}^{--}

[
147
]
.
In the remainder of this section, we focus on the emergence of NDD
in chiral systems.

III.2.2
Electrochirality

Electrochirality gives rise to
magnetochiral dichroism

[
148
,
149
,
150
]
, which is the version of NDD
characterized by the asymmetric absorption of unpolarized light
parallel and antiparallel to an applied magnetic field. This
phenomenon is currently attracting great interest
[
151
]
. It
arises because an electrochiral system subject to a magnetic field
or a magnetization has the reduced symmetries required for a nonzero
tensor
T_{1}^{--}

[
147
,
152
]
.
This effect has equal magnitude but opposite sign for the two
enantiomorphs and can therefore be used to differentiate between
opposite enantiomorphs.

Electrochiral systems are also optically active
[
51
]
, and
they show a linear current-induced magnetization
[
153
,
154
,
6
]
. While these effects do not occur exclusively due to
electrochirality
[
13
,
45
,
155
]
, the magnitude of these
effects is again equal for the two enantiomorphs, but has the
opposite sign.

III.2.3
Magnetochirality

Directional dichroism also arises when a magnetochiral system is
placed into an electric field
[
147
,
156
,
152
,
157
]
.
This
electrochiral dichroism
is characterized by an asymmetry
in the absorption of light parallel and antiparallel to the applied
electric field (or electric polarization). It can be considered as
the dual of magnetochiral dichroism discussed above. Again, the
asymmetry exhibited by the two magnetochiral enantiomorphs is equal
in magnitude but has the opposite sign.

III.2.4
Antimagnetochirality

In antimagnetochiral materials that allow
T_{1}^{--}
, NDD occurs
with equal magnitude and opposite sign for the two enantiomorphs, thus
enabling their experimental differentiation
[
52
,
158
]
. Placing an
antimagnetochiral material into an electric field generates the symmetry
conditions for both the Faraday effect and magnetic circular dichroism to
occur
[
159
]
, with the associated nonreciprocities again
having opposite sign for the two enantiomorphs
[
160
]
.

III.2.5
Multichirality

Multichiral enantiomorphs are more profoundly different than
unichiral enantiomorphs. For physical quantities and effects, it is
generally the
sign
that can be opposite for different chiral
domains. Therefore, to distinguish between all four enantiomorphs of
a multichiral system, more than one physical quantity or effect must
be considered.
For example, multichiral systems show NDD when placed in a magnetic
or an electric field
[
152
]
, thus combining the experimental
signatures of electrochiral and magnetochiral systems. Based on the
sign of the directional dichroisms exhibited in applied magnetic and
electric fields, the four multichiral enantiomorphs can be uniquely
identified.

III.3
Chirality and band structure

As discussed in Sec.
II
, the spin-dependent
electronic band structure
E_{\sigma}({\bm{\mathrm{k}}})
directly reflects the
categories of polarizations in solids. These categories manifest
themselves via characteristic terms in the band structure that
transform like irreducible tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
. In the
following, we argue that the band structure
E_{\sigma}({\bm{\mathrm{k}}})

generally reflects the categories of chirality in solids via an

interplay
of parapolar and unipolar (i.e., electropolar,
magnetopolar, or antimagnetopolar) terms.

The categories of chirality can be defined based on which
monopoles
T_{0}^{ss^{\prime}}
are permitted in a system, see
Table
4
. For example, in electrochiral crystal
structures, the electrotoroidal compound scalar
T_{0}^{-+}
becomes
symmetry allowed. In a band structure, such a scalar is realized by
a term
{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
. The presence of this scalar in a
band structure has thus been identified as a key feature of
electrochiral systems
[
48
]
. It can be viewed as a minimal
model of an effective low-energy Hamiltonian for electrochirality in
solids.

In contrast, using the same building blocks
k,\sigma
, the charges

T_{0}^{+-}
and
T_{0}^{--}
have no band-structure indicators
associated with them (see Table
5
). In
magnetochiral systems (fictitious or engineered), we can envision
compound scalars
T_{0}^{+-}\propto{\bm{\mathrm{d}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
and in
antimagnetochiral systems compound scalars
T_{0}^{--}\propto{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{d}}}
, where
{\bm{\mathrm{d}}}
is an electric dipole
density (signature
-+
) similar to the magnetic dipole density

{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}
(signature
+-
)
[
27
]
.

However, for a crystal structure to be chiral, it is not decisive
whether (and how) chiral charges
T_{0}^{ss^{\prime}}
are actually realized.
Electropolarizations, magnetopolarizations, and
antimagnetopolarizations
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
with
\ell>0
combined
with parapolarizations
T_{\ell}^{++}
with
\ell>0
can likewise
make a system chiral. In the following, we show how tensors

T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
with
\ell>0
can be used to define minimal models
of effective low-energy Hamiltonians for electrochirality,
magnetochirality and antimagnetochirality for all
magnetic-point-group classes
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})

[
132
]
.

Our theory is illustrated in Table
7
, which lists
the lowest-degree invariant tensors realizing chiralities in the
continuous classes
L(R)
and
L(D_{\infty})
, the cubic classes

L(O)
and
L(T)
, and the axial classes
L(D_{n})
and
L(C_{n})
with

n=2,4,3,6

[
132
]
.

Figure 7:
Chirality exhibited in the band
structure for unipolar-unichiral groups in classes
L(R)

(left columns),
L(O)
(center columns), and
L(T)
(right
columns)
[
132
]
. The first row shows energy
surfaces of a parachiral system, whereas the second, third and
fourth rows show energy surfaces for the two enantiomorphs allowed
under the indicated electrochiral, magnetochiral and antimagnetochiral
groups.

Figure 8:
Unipolarizations giving rise to
unichirality in classes
L(T)
(Fig.
7
)
and
L(C_{4})
(Fig.
9
) may also exist in
systems with point groups in classes
L(O)
and

L(D_{4})

[
132
]
, respectively, where these
terms, as shown here,
do not
give rise to unichirality.

Figure 9:
Chirality exhibited in the band
structure for unipolar-unichiral groups in classes

L(D_{\infty})
(left columns),
L(D_{4})
(center columns), and

L(C_{4})
(right columns)
[
132
]
. The first row
shows energy surfaces of a parachiral system, whereas the second,
third and fourth rows show energy surfaces for the two enantiomorphs
allowed under the indicated electrochiral, magnetochiral and
antimagnetochiral groups.

III.3.1
Spherical class
L(R)

The full rotation group
R_{i\times\theta}
is parachiral. The only invariant
tensor permitted by this group is
T_{0}^{++}
that is realized by the
scalar
k^{2}
and functions thereof [Fig
7
(a)].
The electrochiral spherical group
R_{\theta}
also permits a tensor

T_{0}^{-+}\propto{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
(and functions thereof).
The latter term gives rise to two distinct
enantiomorphs
[
161
,
18
,
162
]
, see
Fig
7
(d).
As discussed before, the magnetochiral group
R_{i}
and the
antimagnetochiral group
R_{i\theta}
have no band-structure
indicators associated with them.

III.3.2
Cubic crystal system [classes
L(O)
and
L(T)
]

Beyond the isotropic terms already permitted by the groups in the
spherical class
L(R)
, all groups in the cubic class
L(O)
also
permit an electric hexadecapolarization (
\ell=4
) that manifests
itself in lowest order via a term
[
18
]

H^{(\mathrm{e},4)}\propto k_{x}^{2}k_{y}^{2}+k_{y}^{2}k_{z}^{2}+k_{z}^{2}k_{x}^{2}\,.

(8)

This term represents a warping of the energy dispersion
E_{\sigma}({\bm{\mathrm{k}}})
of band electrons in cubic crystal structures

[
163
,
164
]
, see Fig
7
(b).

The group
O\times C_{\theta}
is electrochiral. In lowest order

\ell
, it permits an electropolarization with
\ell=9
that gives
rise to a term

\displaystyle H^{(\mathrm{e},9)}\propto{}

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}k_{x}[k_{y}^{2}k_{z}^{2}(3k_{y}^{4}-10k_{y}^{2}k_{z}^{2}+3k_{z}^{4})

\displaystyle+k_{x}^{2}(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})(k_{y}^{4}-6k_{y}^{2}k_{z}^{2}+k_{z}^{4})]+\mbox{cp}\,,

(9)

see Fig.
7
(e). The electrotoroidal scalar

{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
that is generally likewise present in such
systems can be interpreted as a compound moment density
(
4
) due to the combined effect of the
electropolarization (
9
) and parapolarizations
including the hexadecapolarization (
8
).

By SIS-TIS duality, the group
O\times C_{i}
is magnetochiral. In
lowest order
\ell
, it permits a magnetopolarization with
\ell=9
that gives rise to a term

\displaystyle H^{(\mathrm{m},9)}\propto{}

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}k_{y}k_{z}(k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})[5k_{x}^{4}-3k_{x}^{2}(k_{y}^{2}+k_{z}^{2})+k_{y}^{2}k_{z}^{2}]

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt+\mbox{cp}\,,

(10)

see Fig.
7
(g). Finally, the group
O\times C_{i\theta}
is antimagnetochiral. It permits an antimagnetopolarization
(
\ell=0
) that manifests itself, in lowest order
\ell
, via a
magnetotoroidal density with
\ell=9
that gives rise to a term

\displaystyle H^{(\mathrm{am},9)}\propto{}

\displaystyle k_{x}k_{y}k_{z}(k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})(k_{z}^{2}-k_{x}^{2})(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\,,

(11)

see Fig.
7
(i).

Besides class
L(O)
, the cubic crystal system also includes class

L(T)
. As
T\subset O
, the polarizations permitted by the
point groups
G_{O}
in
L(O)
are also permitted by the respective
groups
G_{T}\subset G_{O}
in
L(T)
. Beyond that, the groups in

L(T)
permit polarizations not present in
L(O)
, as listed in
Table
7
. These terms are of lower order than the
terms permitted by
L(O)
.

Specifically, the electrochiral group
T\times C_{\theta}
permits an
electric octupolarization
\ell=3
that manifests itself via a
Dresselhaus term

H^{(\mathrm{e},3)}\propto\sigma_{x}k_{x}(k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})+\mbox{cp}\,.

(12)

As is well known
[
103
]
, this term is already allowed for
systems with the parachiral point group
T_{d}\times C_{\theta}=O[T]\times C_{\theta}
[Fig.
8
(a)] realized, e.g., by
the zincblende structure; i.e., by itself, this term is not
sufficient as an indicator for electrochirality in class
L(T)
.
The Dresselhaus term (
12
) becomes an indicator
for electrochirality [Fig.
7
(f)] when combined
with the parapolarization [
\ell=6
,
Fig.
7
(c)]

H^{(\mathrm{e},6)}\propto(k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})(k_{z}^{2}-k_{x}^{2})(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\,.

(13)

The parapolarization (
13
) is forbidden by the
groups in class
L(O)
, but it is allowed in class
L(T)
. A
parapolarization with
\ell=6
that is allowed already in class

L(O)
is listed in Table
7
.

Similarly, an altermagnetic magnetopolarization (
\ell=3
)

H^{(\mathrm{m},3)}\propto\sigma_{x}k_{y}k_{z}+\mbox{cp}

(14)

may exist in systems with magnetochiral group
T\times C_{i}
, but
also in parachiral systems with point group
O(T)\times C_{i}
. The
magnetopolarization (
14
) becomes an indicator for
magnetochirality when combined with the parapolarization
(
13
), see Fig.
7
(h).
Likewise, an antimagnetopolarization yields a magnetotoroidal
term with
\ell=3

H^{(\mathrm{am},3)}\propto k_{x}k_{y}k_{z}\,.

(15)

This term may exist in systems with antimagnetochiral group
T\times C_{i\theta}
, but also in parachiral systems with point group

O(T)\times C_{i\theta}
. The term (
15
) becomes
an indicator for antimagnetochirality when combined with the
parapolarization (
13
), see
Fig.
7
(j).

To summarize, in class
L(T)
, the unipolar terms
(
12
), (
14
), and
(
15
) become indicators of electrochirality,
magnetochirality, and antimagnetochirality, respectively, only when
each of these terms is combined with the parapolarization
(
13
); i.e., jointly, the terms define minimal
models for effective low-energy Hamiltonians realizing
electrochirality (point group
T\times C_{\theta}
), magnetochirality
(
T\times C_{i}
), and antimagnetochirality (
T\times C_{i\theta}
),
respectively. This is discussed further in
Sec.
III.3.4
.

III.3.3
Axial systems [classes
L(D_{n})
and
L(C_{n})
]

Similar to the preceding discussion of the cubic classes
L(O)
and

L(T)
, Table
7
also lists the lowest-order
parapolar and unipolar tensors permitted by class
L(D_{\infty})
,
as well as classes
L(D_{n})
and
L(C_{n})
with
n=2,4,3,6
.
Figure
9
illustrates the chiral band structure for

L(D_{\infty})
,
L(D_{4})
, and
L(C_{4})
. The corresponding set of
plots for
L(D_{3})
is shown in Fig.
3
. For the dihedral
classes
L(D_{6})
,
L(D_{3})
,
L(D_{4})
, and
L(D_{2})
unichirality is
realized by combining suitable parapolar terms with unipolar terms
with
\ell=7
, 3, 5, and 3, respectively. Note that
D_{2}\subset T
,
so class
L(D_{2})
largely reproduces the features of class
L(T)
.

For magnetochiral
\alpha
-
Fe
2
O
3
and antimagnetochiral
Cr
2
O
3
depicted in Fig.
3
, the joint presence
of an electric hexadecapolarization and a magnetic (for

\alpha
-
Fe
2
O
3
) or a magnetotoroidal (for Cr
2
O
3
)
octupolarization generates chirality in the band structure. A
hypothetical electrochiral corundum derivative (second column of
Fig.
3
) would similarly be characterized by having
both the electric hexadecapolarization and an electric
octupolarization present.

The electropolar cyclic groups
C_{n}\times C_{\theta}
permit a
spontaneous electric dipolarization (
\ell=1
) that manifests
itself via a Rashba term

H^{(\mathrm{e},1)}\propto\sigma_{x}k_{y}-\sigma_{y}k_{x}\,.

(16)

As is well known, this term is already allowed for systems with
parachiral point groups
C_{nv}\times C_{\theta}=D_{n}[C_{n}]\times C_{\theta}
realized, e.g., by the wurtzite structure (
C_{6v}\times C_{\theta}
)
[
102
]
. See also Fig.
8
(b).
When the Rashba term is combined with a suitable parapolar term
forbidden by class
L(D_{n})
, but allowed by class
L(C_{n})

(see Table
7
), jointly these terms describe an
electrochiral system [Fig.
9
(f)].

Similarly, the cyclic groups
C_{n}\times C_{i}
are magnetopolar;
i.e., they permit a spontaneous magnetization (
\ell=1
) that
manifests itself via an exchange (Zeeman) term

H^{(\mathrm{m},1)}\propto\sigma_{z}\,.

(17)

Such a term is already allowed for systems with the parachiral point
groups
D_{n}(C_{n})\times C_{i}
, see Fig.
8
(d).
This term becomes an indicator of magnetochirality when combined
with the respective parapolar terms listed for classes
L(C_{n})

in Table
7
[Fig.
9
(h)].

Finally, the cyclic groups
C_{n}\times C_{i\theta}
are antimagnetopolar,
i.e., they permit a spontaneous antimagnetopolarization that
manifests itself via a magnetotoroidal term (
\ell=1
)

H^{(\mathrm{am},1)}\propto k_{z}\,.

(18)

Again, such a term is already allowed for systems with the
parachiral point groups
D_{n}(C_{n})\times C_{i\theta}
, see
Fig.
8
(f). This term becomes an indicator of
antimagnetochirality when combined with the respective parapolar
terms listed for classes
L(C_{n})
in
Table
7
[Fig.
9
(j)].

III.3.4
Minimal effective models for chirality

For each tabulated tensor, Table
7
lists its
maximal group, i.e., the largest group that leaves the tensor
invariant. For the odd
-
\ell
electropolar, magnetopolar and
antimagnetopolar tensors, the respective maximal group is always an
improper group beyond class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, so these tensors,
by themselves, cannot serve as minimal effective models for
chirality in class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, as illustrated in
Fig.
8
.

Quite generally, a proper point group
G_{\mathrm{p}}
is an invariant
subgroup of
i
-
minor groups
\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}[G_{\mathrm{p}}]

G_{\mathrm{p}}\subset\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}[G_{\mathrm{p}}]\;,

(19a)

see Appendix
A.2
. Specifically, we have (
n=1,2,3,\ldots
)

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\subset T_{d}=O[T]

(19b)

\displaystyle D_{2n}

\displaystyle\subset D_{(2n)d}=D_{4n}[D_{2n}]

(19c)

\displaystyle D_{2n+1}

\displaystyle\subset D_{(2n+1)h}=D_{4n+2}[D_{2n+1}]

(19d)

\displaystyle C_{2n}

\displaystyle\subset\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}S_{4n}&=C_{4n}[C_{2n}]\\
C_{(2n)v}&=D_{2n}[C_{2n}]\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle C_{2n+1}

\displaystyle\subset\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}C_{(2n+1)h}&=C_{4n+2}[C_{2n+1}]\\
C_{(2n+1)v}&=D_{2n+1}[C_{2n+1}]\end{array}\right.

and similarly for the supergroups
\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}(G_{\mathrm{p}})
and
\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\{G_{\mathrm{p}}\}
of
G_{\mathrm{p}}
. Of course, while a group like
D_{4}
is a
crystallographic point group, the supergroup
D_{4d}=D_{8}[D_{4}]\supset D_{4}
is not a crystallographic point group. Nonetheless,
the improper point group
D_{4d}
is, ignoring TIS, the maximal
group for the lowest-degree electropolar term permitted by the
crystallographic point group
D_{4}
, see
Table
7
. So this electropolar term, by
itself, cannot serve as a minimal effective model for
electrochirality in class
L(D_{4})
.

Among the finite point groups, the octahedral group
O
is special
as it does not appear as a subgroup of a finite group outside class
L(O)
. Therefore, the unipolar tensors with
\ell=9

listed in Table
7
for class
L(O)

represent, by themselves, minimal effective models for unichirality
in class
L(O)
.

While the maximal groups for the unipolar tensors listed in
Table
7
generally go beyond the respective
class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, for each of the parapolar tensors in
Table
7
the maximal group is the full group

G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i\times\theta}
of the respective class. Therefore,

jointly
the unipolar tensors combined with the respective
parapolar tensors represent minimal, effective models for
unichirality in class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. Multichirality is realized
by combining at least two different unipolar tensors in a class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
.

The cooperation of a parapolarization and a unipolarization to
realize unichirality can also be recognized in
Fig.
4
. In Fig.
4
(e)
we can envision that polar properties have been added to the
parapolar nonchiral object
T_{\ell 0}^{++}
. Alternatively, we
could imagine that the unipolar nonchiral objects
T_{u0}^{-+}
,

T_{u0}^{+-}
, and
T_{u0}^{--}
have acquired a second nonpolar
property, so jointly these properties realize chirality. These
examples illustrate that we can generally have multiple pathways to
realize chirality.

The multipolar-unichiral case in Fig.
6
is
different from the unipolar-unichiral case in
Figs.
4
and
5
. The
objects shown in Fig.
6
(e) visualize the
irreducible tensors
T_{g0}^{++}
and
T_{u0}^{-+}
,
T_{u0}^{+-}
,
and
T_{u0}^{--}
as indicated. While individual objects

T_{u0}^{ss^{\prime}}
in Fig.
6
(e) are unipolar but
not chiral, it is the superposition of their polarizations that
yields unichirality [Figs.
6
(a) to (c)] and
multichirality [Fig.
6
(d)].

This rule applies also to minimal effective models for chirality in
multipolar-unichiral systems. For example, the
multipolar-electrochiral group
D_{4}(C_{4})
permits the electropolar term

\displaystyle H^{(\mathrm{e},5)}\propto{}

\displaystyle\sigma_{x}k_{x}[k_{x}^{2}(k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})-k_{y}^{2}(k_{y}^{2}-3k_{z}^{2})]+(x\leftrightarrow y)

\displaystyle+\sigma_{z}k_{z}(k_{x}^{4}-6k_{x}^{2}k_{y}^{2}+k_{y}^{4})\,,

(20)

[because
D_{4}(C_{4})\subset D_{4}\times C_{\theta}\subset D_{8}[D_{4}]\times C_{\theta}
, see Table
7
and
Appendix
A.3
], the magnetopolar term
(
17
) [because
D_{4}(C_{4})\subset D_{4}(C_{4})\times C_{i}
, see Table
7
], and the antimagnetopolar
term (
18
) [because
D_{4}(C_{4})\subset D_{4}(C_{4})\times C_{i\theta}
]. Any two of these terms are sufficient as a minimal
model for electrochirality under
D_{4}(C_{4})
because the largest
subgroup common to any two of the three groups
D_{4}\times C_{\theta}
,

D_{4}(C_{4})\times C_{i}
, and
D_{4}(C_{4})\times C_{i\theta}
is
D_{4}(C_{4})
. Of course,
D_{4}(C_{4})
permits not only the electropolar
term (
20
), but also the parapolar term

H^{(\mathrm{e},4)}\propto k_{x}^{2}k_{y}^{2}

(21)

because
D_{4}(C_{4})\subset D_{4}\times C_{i\times\theta}
, see
Table
7
. Jointly, the terms
(
20
) and (
21
) can serve not
only as a minimal model for electrochirality under
D_{4}\times C_{\theta}
, but also under
D_{4}(C_{4})\subset D_{4}\times C_{\theta}
.

Using Table
7
, minimal models for other
multipolar-unichiral systems can be constructed in a similar way.
Thus, we have a complete theory for minimal effective models
describing chirality in crystals
[
101
,
165
]
.

III.4
Induced chirality

From a symmetry point of view, an electropolar system with
spontaneous electric dipolarization (
16
) is
equivalent to a parapolar system placed into an external electric
field. In parapolar systems with crystallographic point group
C_{n}\times C_{i\times\theta}
, electrochirality can therefore be induced by
placing the system into an external electric field
[
28
]
.
Similarly, magnetochirality can be induced by placing such a system
into an external magnetic field. Finally, parallel electric and
magnetic fields make such systems multichiral; i.e., they not only induce
a Rashba term (
16
) and a Zeeman term
(
17
), but, jointly, the fields also induce a
magnetotoroidal term (
18
).

IV
Conclusions and outlook

Our work introduces a standard model of electromagnetism and
chirality in crystals that provides a comprehensive classification
of broken symmetries and the physical properties they give rise to
in all 122 magnetic crystal classes. The standard model is based on
five categories of electric and magnetic multipole order and five
categories of chirality that yield 12 types of crystal structures
(see Fig.
1
), each with distinctive electric
and magnetic properties. Multiple versions of chirality that were
previously overlooked, including multichirality with four distinct
enantiomorphs, are novel rubrics underlying our complete
classification. Our work shows explicitly how chirality is linked
to electromagnetic order and band structure. In particular, we show
how each category of chirality arises from distinct superpositions
of electric and magnetic multipole densities. We also discuss
band-structure indicators of chirality. For this purpose, we provide a
complete theory of minimal effective models characterizing the
different categories of chirality in different systems. These
minimal models demonstrate explicitly how chirality in crystalline
solids requires the interplay of multiple polarizations, either the
interplay of unipolar and parapolar terms, or the interplay of
multiple unipolar terms.

IV.1
Macroscopic description of multipole order

An overarching goal of our work is to provide a general theory of
electric and magnetic order in crystals that is freed from any
reference to microscopic gauge-dependent degrees of freedom. To achieve this goal, we are generalizing the fundamental ideas developed earlier to
discuss electric
[
12
,
34
]
and magnetic
[
20
,
21
,
34
]
dipole order to higher-rank multipoles. The five
categories of multipole order (i.e., polarizations; see columns in
Fig.
1
) subsume phenomenology-based materials
classifications and directly reflect distinctive features in the
electronic band structure (Fig.
2
).

We present a careful and comprehensive discussion of how compound
multipoles arise in crystals, going beyond previous descriptions
that utilized polarized harmonics
[
23
,
24
,
88
]
.
These insights underpin the explanation for how
antimagnetopolarizations (i.e., even
-
\ell
magnetic-multipole
densities) are always represented by odd
-
\ell

magnetotoroidal terms in the spin-degenerate electronic band
structure.

IV.2
Hidden order parameters

The electric and magnetic dipole densities
\ell=1
(i.e.,
electric dipolarization and magnetization) represent the familiar
order parameters in the thermodynamic description of ferroelectrics
and ferromagnets, respectively
[
51
]
. Beyond these dipole
densities
\ell=1
in ferroelectric and ferromagnetic media,
multipole densities with rank
\ell>1
can serve as physically
transparent order parameters of hidden orders in crystalline
matter, thus facilitating their experimental detection
[
166
,
167
,
64
]
. To this end, our work establishes a direct
association of multipole densities with the 122 magnetic crystal
classes, as illustrated in Fig.
2
. Generally,
electric and magnetic multipole densities with odd
\ell>1

represent alterelectrics and altermagnets, respectively, i.e.,
electric and magnetic orders without a polar direction, while the
even
-
\ell
antimagnetopolar (and odd
-
\ell
magnetotoroidal)
multipole densities describe antimagnetopolar media. Application of
methods developed in this work enables the absolute and
gauge-invariant quantification of electric and magnetic multipole
densities of any rank
[
36
]
, which is a precondition for any
well-defined order parameter
[
37
,
38
]
.

Using multipole densities as order parameters presents an attractive
alternative to utilizing staggered versions of electric or magnetic
dipoles represented, e.g., by a Néel vector
[
51
,
168
]
.
For example, the fundamental physical differences between
magnetopolar antiferromagnets (i.e., altermagnets) and
antimagnetopolar antiferromagnets (e.g., magnetoelectrics) are
naturally captured by the different multipole densities representing
these orders, which also allows for clear association with
macroscopic physical properties (such as the distinctive
band-structure features shown in Fig.
2
). Making such
connections based on Néel-vector constructions is typically less
straightforward
[
169
,
170
]
.

IV.3
Response tensors

In the present work, we have focused on
irreducible
tensors,
i.e., we have focused on tensors that transform irreducibly under
point groups, including the spherical rotation groups and the
crystallographic point groups. These irreducible tensors naturally
lend themselves to the description of crystal
properties
such
as the multipole densities that have been a central topic of the
present work. We note that Cartesian tensors that are commonly
reducible find widespread use as material tensors that describe
crystal
responses
to external perturbations
[
13
,
96
]
.
Recent works have studied nonequilibrium effects such as
spin-dependent transport
[
171
,
172
]
, and light-tailored
multipole order
[
173
,
174
,
175
]
. A thorough analysis of
such effects needs to consider Onsager’s reciprocal relations
accounting for the symmetry of fluctuations under time inversion

\theta
when studying irreversible processes
[
38
]
. In the
context of magnetic systems breaking TIS, such an analysis is not
trivial. For example, following early work
[
176
]
, the
symmetry of the conductivity tensor describing the anomalous Hall
effect in magnetically ordered systems was discussed intensely till
a consensus was reached. See Ref.
[
116
]
. A proper
discussion of this important point is beyond the scope of the
present work. It will be the subject of a future publication.

IV.4
More chirality from general improper rotations

The present work generalizes the concept of chirality, or
enantiomorphism, to include noninvariance under improper rotations
involving any one (or all three) among the inversions
i
,
\theta

and
i\theta
, leading to the identification of five categories of
chirality (rows in Fig.
1
, see also
Fig.
3
). This categorization settles previous
arguments
[
43
,
45
,
136
]
about the place of TIS in
definitions of chirality. We furthermore advance the search for
band-structure indicators of enantiomorphism, presenting minimal
models for each category of chirality realized in specific crystal
classes (see Sec.
III.3
, especially
Table
7
). Our findings make it evident that
there is no universal indicator that is both necessary and
sufficient for identifying chirality in all crystal classes.
Rather, each unichiral or multichiral crystal class has its own
interplay between parapolar and unipolar multipole densities that
has its distinct fingerprints in the electronic band structure. The
often-touted Dirac-like spin splitting term proportional to
{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
arises in electrochiral systems simply as a remnant of
the electrotoroidal scalar
T_{0}^{-+}
having the Dirac term as an
indicator already under the electrochiral rotation group
R_{\theta}
.

The newly proposed (anti
-
) magnetochiral and multichiral
categories will be of particular interest for further experimental
exploration. In Sec.
III.2.3
, we describe the
unusual nonreciprocal effects associated with magnetochiral systems
(e.g., electrochiral dichroism), which have started to be
explored experimentally
[
157
]
. Previous studies of
multichiral systems focused on materials that are also
multiferroic
[
146
]
, where effects due to enantiomorphism are
masked by multiferroic responses. We encourage future experimental
investigations to focus on materials from the crystal classes
D_{2}
,

D_{4}
,
D_{3}
,
D_{6}
,
T
or
O
[see Table
23(d)
],
as the various nonreciprocities expected from multichirality should
be more pristinely exhibited in these classes that do not support a
polar direction.

also benefited from discussions with B. Assaf, S. Bey, T. Birol,
L. Buiarelli, R. Cavanaugh, A. Hoffmann, N. Ghimire, L. Lurio, and
J. Rau. U.Z. would like to thank S. Marsland for useful comments
on an early version of the manuscript. Work at Argonne was
supported by Department of Energy, Office of Basic Energy Sciences
under Contract No. DE-AC02-06CH11357.

Data availability

All data used for the research described in the article have been
included in the article.

Appendix A
Composition of magnetic point groups

The symmetry of a crystal structure is fully characterized by its
(magnetic) space group
S
, which includes point symmetries,
translations, and combinations thereof
[
10
]
. However,
according to Neumann’s principle
[
12
,
13
,
14
]
,
macroscopic properties of a crystal structure such as material
tensors and electric and magnetic polarization densities that may be
realized in a structure depend only on the magnetic
point
group

G
associated with the space group
S
. We therefore focus
on point groups
G
in this work. For crystal structures
transforming according to a symmorphic space group
S_{\mathrm{s}}
,
the point group
G
is the finite subgroup of
S_{\mathrm{s}}

consisting of the elements of
S_{\mathrm{s}}
that leave one point in
space fixed. Nonsymmorphic space groups
S_{\mathrm{n}}
also contain
group elements that combine point-group symmetries
g
with
nonprimitive translations. Here the elements
g
are also elements
of the point group
G
, although these symmetry operations are not,
by themselves, elements of
S_{\mathrm{n}}
. The latter case makes
crystallographic point groups defining crystal classes qualitatively
distinct from point groups of finite systems like molecules.

This appendix reviews the basic structure and properties of magnetic
point groups and introduces a new classification scheme where SIS
and TIS are systematically treated on the same footing. The
relationships revealed through this classification underpin our
comprehensive discussion of multipole order
(Sec.
II
) and chirality (Sec.
III
)
in crystals.

A.1
Black-white symmetries and inversion groups

Rotation symmetries are represented by proper point groups

G_{\mathrm{p}}
that only contain proper rotations as symmetry elements.
In contrast, SIS and TIS are both examples of so-called

black-white
symmetries
[
177
,
11
,
178
]
, which are
represented by two-element groups of the form

C_{\gamma}=\{e,\gamma\}\quad.

(22)

Here
e
is the identity, and
\gamma
is the single nontrivial
group element that transforms between two qualities, represented
abstractly by “black” and “white,” satisfying
\gamma^{2}=e
.
Space inversion
i
is such an operation, as is time inversion

\theta
(for our systems of interest
[
17
]
), and
their combination
i\theta
(CIS). We use the symbols
C_{i}
,

C_{\theta}
, and
C_{i\theta}
to refer to the inversion groups
C_{\gamma}

defined in Eq. (
22
) when
\gamma=i
,
\theta
, and

i\theta
, respectively. For completeness and notational
simplicity, we also define the full inversion group
C_{i\times\theta}\equiv C_{i}\times C_{\theta}=\{e,i,\theta,i\theta\}
and the trivial
inversion group
C_{1}=\{e\}
. These groups are summarized in
Table
1
. In the present work, the symbol

C_{\zeta}
stands for any of the five inversion groups
C_{i\times\theta}
,

C_{\theta}
,
C_{i}
,
C_{i\theta}
, or
C_{1}
.

A.2
Combining a black-white symmetry with other symmetries

There are three possible types of point groups
G
that arise from
the combination of a black-white symmetry
\gamma
with other
symmetries. As we consider multiple black-white symmetries in this
work, we make their definitions specific:

(a)

The
\gamma
-
lacking
groups
G^{(\mathrm{a})}
do
not contain
\gamma
at all.

(b)

The
\gamma
-
major
groups are of the form

G^{(\mathrm{b})}=G^{(\mathrm{a})}\times C_{\gamma}\quad,

(23)

so
\gamma
is, by itself, an element of
G^{(\mathrm{b})}
.

(c)

Suppose a
\gamma
-
lacking group
G^{(\mathrm{a})}

contains an invariant subgroup of index 2 denoted

\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}
, i.e.,

G^{(\mathrm{a})}=\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}+\bar{g}\,\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}\quad,

(24a)

where
\bar{g}\in G^{(\mathrm{a})}
, but
\bar{g}\notin\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}
. Then, the
\gamma
-
minor

group is defined as

G^{(\mathrm{c})}\equiv G^{(\mathrm{a})}\langle\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}\rangle_{\gamma}=\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}+\gamma\bar{g}\,\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}\quad.

(24b)

By definition, the major group
G^{(\mathrm{a})}\times C_{\gamma}
contains
the
\gamma
-
lacking group
G^{(\mathrm{a})}
and any
minor group
G^{(\mathrm{a})}\langle\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}\rangle_{\gamma}
as subgroups;

\displaystyle G^{(\mathrm{a})}

\displaystyle\subset G^{(\mathrm{a})}\times C_{\gamma}\,\,,

(25a)

\displaystyle G^{(\mathrm{a})}\langle\tilde{G}^{(\mathrm{a})}\rangle_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\subset G^{(\mathrm{a})}\times C_{\gamma}\,\,.

(25b)

We adopt the convention established in the context of incorporating TIS,
whereby, for
\gamma=\theta
, the resulting groups
G^{(\mathrm{a})}
,

G^{(\mathrm{b})}
and
G^{(\mathrm{c})}
have been referred to as type
I, type II and type III, respectively
[
177
,
51
]
, with the
particular type
-
III notation

\gamma=\theta:\qquad G(\tilde{G})\equiv G\langle\tilde{G}\rangle_{\theta}\quad.

(26a)

Similarly, we label groups
G^{(\mathrm{a})}
,
G^{(\mathrm{b})}

and
G^{(\mathrm{c})}
arising in the process of incorporating
SIS, i.e., when
\gamma=i
, as type i, type ii and type iii,
respectively, and denote type
-
iii groups by

\gamma=i:\qquad G[\tilde{G}]\equiv G\langle\tilde{G}\rangle_{i}\quad.

(26b)

Groups that contain both
i
and
\theta
can also be classified
as major or minor groups with respect to
i\theta
. We indicate

i\theta
-
minor groups by

\gamma=i\theta:\qquad G\{\tilde{G}\}\equiv G\langle\tilde{G}\rangle_{i\theta}\quad.

(26c)

See Table
8
for a summary of our nomenclature
and specifics of notation.

,

with other symmetries. The

A.3
Classes and types of magnetic point groups; duality

Proper point groups
G_{\mathrm{p}}
are both
i
-
lacking and

\theta
-
lacking, and thus also
i\theta
-
lacking. Combining
these with SIS using the formalism described in
Appendix
A.2
yields the ordinary improper point
groups
[
15
,
11
]
that can be either
i
-
major or

i
-
minor. These groups are still
\theta
-
lacking and

i\theta
-
lacking. Combining these groups with TIS in the same
way yields the magnetic point groups
[
19
]
. In this process
of extending the proper point groups by adding SIS and TIS, each
proper point group
G_{\mathrm{p}}
spawns a
class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
of
magnetic point groups
G

[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
]
defined
via

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})=\{G:G\mapsto G_{\mathrm{p}}\;\mbox{when}\;i,\theta\mapsto e\}\;.

(27)

By definition, each magnetic point group
G
belongs to one and only
one class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. We call the proper point group
G_{\mathrm{p}}
the

class root
of class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
.

are positioned underneath the self-dual type with which they form
a triad. In addition to the short type label, we provide a full
label indicating the types with respect to SIS (i, ii or iii), TIS
(I, II or III) and CIS (J, JJ or JJJ). Small
2\times 2
matrices mark congruences between tensors with signature
ss^{\prime}

as discussed in detail in Appendix
E
. The
categories of multipole order (i.e., polarization) and chirality
are listed for each type.

Subgroup relations and
isomorphisms between groups in a given class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. Subgroup relations are depicted by lines. Groups
placed within the same row are isomorphic. The categories of
polarization and chirality for each group are also indicated, with the
prefix “uni” representing “electro”, “magneto”, or “antimagneto”.
Numbers
n
at the lower right of a box specify how many groups
represented by a box can be realized for inversion symmetries

\gamma,\bar{\gamma}=i
,
\theta
, and
i\theta
with

\bar{\gamma}\neq\gamma
. When
n=1
, these groups are
self-triadic. When
n=3
, these groups form a triad [Eq. (
31
)]. When a line connects groups that depend on

\gamma,\bar{\gamma}
, the subgroup relation exists pairwise
between the groups in the triads with the same
\gamma,\bar{\gamma}
. Every class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
contains the groups
shown in the left (dark-blue) column. If
G_{\mathrm{p}}
has an
index
-
2 subgroup
\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}
, the class also includes
the groups in the center (medium-blue) column. If

\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}
has an index
-
2 subgroup

\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}
, the class also includes the groups in
the right (light-blue) column. Below the dividing line at the
bottom, subgroup relations between groups in class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
and the groups
\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}
and

\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}
are shown. See also
Table
9
that presents the groups that may exist
in a class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
in a more fine-grained way by
indicating their types.

The separate consideration of the two black-white properties
i
and

\theta
yields overlapping group types, i.e., the full set of
magnetic point groups can be viewed in terms of TIS as a collection
of types I, II and III, or seen equivalently
[
55
,
56
]
in
terms of SIS as a collection of types i, ii and iii. Yet another
classification can be based on CIS. We now develop a scheme that
treats SIS and TIS symmetrically and makes CIS explicit, thus
providing a systematic and complete classification of the magnetic
point groups. This approach also reveals a general structure
underlying the composition of class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
of
magnetic point groups with class root
G_{\mathrm{p}}
.

It has previously been observed
[
55
]
that exchanging
i\leftrightarrow\theta
in all group elements of a magnetic point
group
G
yields another magnetic point group
G_{\mathrm{d}}
.
Clearly, the groups
G
and
G_{\mathrm{d}}
are isomorphic. We call
this relationship
SIS-TIS duality
or
i\leftrightarrow\theta

duality

G\;\leftrightarrow\;G_{\mathrm{d}}

(28)

with
dual partners

G
and
G_{\mathrm{d}}
. It is easy to see
that dual partners
G
and
G_{\mathrm{d}}
belong to the same class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, i.e., both
G
and
G_{\mathrm{d}}
satisfy Eq. (
27
) with the same class root
G_{\mathrm{p}}
. The
concept of duality allows one to classify the groups in a given
class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
as follows.

Self-dual
groups
G=G_{\mathrm{d}}\in L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
are
invariant under exchange
i\leftrightarrow\theta
. They belong to
one (and only one) of the seven types
,
,

,
,
,
, or

defined in Table
9
.
Besides the self-dual types, we have types associated with dual pairs

G\;\leftrightarrow\;G_{\mathrm{d}}
with
G\neq G_{\mathrm{d}}
and
G,G_{\mathrm{d}}\in L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. We label these types by indicating
their respective types under both SIS and TIS. See Table
9

for the explicit definitions. In the following, we generally use short
labels when referring to one of the new types, but an instructive full
label indicating their types under SIS, TIS and CIS is also given in
Table
9
.

Within each class, the groups of the types
,

,
,
,
,

,
, and
are
all isomorphic. Furthermore, the groups of the types
,

,
,
,
,
and
are all isomorphic. A group of type

is not isomorphic to any other group in the same
class but contains all of these as its subgroups. See
Fig.
10
for a description of general isomorphisms
and subgroup relations between magnetic-point-group types in a
given class. Isomorphisms formed the basis of a prior
classification scheme of the 122 magnetic crystallographic
groups
[
57
,
58
]
.

Table
9
provides comprehensive information
about all magnetic-point-group types, including associated categories of
multipole order and chirality. These latter properties have been elucidated
in Secs.
II
and
III
. As is already apparent
from Table
9
, the 15 indicated group types map
onto particular combinations of a category of polarization with a category
of chirality. For 11 types, this mapping is unique, i.e., for each of these,
the group type is synonymous with a distinct combination of polarization
with chirality. The exception is the multipolar-parachiral combination that
subsumes four (rare sub)types
,
,

and
. Thus, Table
9

and Fig.
10
constitute an alternative representation of
the standard model illustrated by Fig.
1
.

The present classification treats
\theta
as a black-white symmetry
like
i
so that
i^{2}=\theta^{2}=e
. This classification applies
to states with integer spin angular momentum (transforming according
to the single groups where
\theta^{2}=e
), and it is relevant for
all macroscopic properties of a crystal structure such as those
characterized by Neumann’s principle
[
12
,
13
,
17
]
. The single-group description is also sufficient for
obtaining the invariant expansion of the electronic band structure
near
k=0

[
14
]
as in Fig.
2
. This is
illustrated by the fact that all building blocks in Eq. (
5
) for forming invariants in a spinful theory
transform according to integer IRs of
R_{i\times\theta}
with
\ell=1
. This
includes the operator of spin angular momentum
{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}
, Eq. (
5c
). There are other properties of systems with
half-integer spin degrees of freedom that can only be properly
discussed using double groups where
\theta^{2}=-e

[
11
]
.
For example, double groups are needed to understand certain
degeneracies in the electronic band structure that may arise at the
Brillouin zone boundary
[
14
]
. The typology and isomorphisms
discussed here do not hold for the respective double groups.

A.4
Triadic relationships

The SIS-TIS duality (
28
) appears naturally when
magnetic point groups are constructed in the usual way from proper
point groups by adding the black-white symmetries space inversion

i
and time inversion
\theta

[
15
,
19
]
as reviewed at
the beginning of Appendix
A.3
. The duality
(
28
) turns out to be useful to discuss physical
phenomena involving electric and magnetic fields, and we make
use of it throughout this work. Here we briefly discuss the deeper
mathematical structure from which this duality emerges.

The full inversion group
C_{i\times\theta}
has order 4. It is isomorphic to the
Klein four-group

K_{4}=\{e,\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime},\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}:\gamma^{\prime\,2}=\gamma^{\prime\prime\,2}=\tilde{\gamma}^{2}=e\}

(29)

in abstract group theory
[
11
]
. This group treats the
elements
\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}
, and
\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
fully
symmetrically. Indeed, one can express the full inversion group

C_{i\times\theta}
in three equivalent forms

C_{i\times\theta}=C_{i}\times C_{\theta}\equiv C_{\theta}\times C_{i\theta}\equiv C_{i\theta}\times C_{i}\,\,.

(30)

Starting from a proper point group
G_{\mathrm{p}}
, it is thus
possible to construct all magnetic point groups in class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
using any pair of inversion symmetries
\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\{i,\theta,i\theta\}
by first adding the
black-white symmetry
\gamma^{\prime}
and then adding
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
.
Therefore, the concept of duality exists for any pair of inversion
symmetries
\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}\in\{i,\theta,i\theta\}
. The
resulting dualities are represented by the diagram

[formula]
[formula]
[formula]

(31)

with
triadic partners

G_{i}
,
G_{\theta}
, and
G_{i\theta}

that are pairwise connected by duality relations similar to
Eq. (
28
).

Triadic partners
G_{i}
,
G_{\theta}
, and
G_{i\theta}
are isomorphic.
A set of triadic partners
G_{i}
,
G_{\theta}
, and
G_{i\theta}

constitutes a
triad
of magnetic groups. Possibilities for
forming triads are shown in Table
9
. (See also
Fig.
1
for an illustration.)

Self-triadic
groups are invariant under all dualities in the
diagram (
31
). They belong to the types
,

, or
. The remaining groups form
triads with distinct partners
G_{i}
,
G_{\theta}
, and
G_{i\theta}

as indicated in Table
9
. The index
\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
of a group
G_{\tilde{\gamma}}
in the diagram
(
31
) indicates self-duality under the exchange

\gamma^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}
. The equivalent expressions
provided in Table
9
for groups
,

,
,
,

,
, and
,
emphasize the respective self-dualities of these groups.

A.5
Subclasses of magnetic point groups

To classify the tensors permitted by different groups
G
in a class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, the concept of subclasses of magnetic point groups
turns out to be useful. Here we discuss the construction of subclasses,
details of their composition, and associated properties.

For
\gamma=i
,
\theta
, and
i\theta
, the
subclasses

L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)
of a class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
are the subsets
of groups
G

L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)=\{G:G\mapsto G_{0}^{\gamma}\;\mbox{when}\;\gamma\mapsto e\}\;.

(32)

For given
\gamma
, the subclasses
L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)
of

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
are disjoint, i.e., each group
G\in L(G_{\mathrm{p}})

belongs to one subclass
L(G_{0}^{i},i)
, one subclass
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
, and one subclass
L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)
. The subclasses
derived for
\gamma=i
,
\theta
, and
i\theta
hence represent
alternative schemes for partitioning
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. Conversely, for
a given class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
and fixed
\gamma=i
,
\theta
, or

i\theta
, the subclasses
L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)
spawn the class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, i.e.,

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})=\bigcup_{G_{0}^{\gamma}}L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)\quad\mbox{for fixed $\gamma=i$, $\theta$ or $i\theta$.}

(33)

The
subclass root

G_{0}^{\gamma}
of the subclass

L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)
is
\gamma
-
lacking by construction. If

G_{0}^{\gamma}=G_{\mathrm{p}}
the subclass root
G_{0}^{\gamma}
is actually
lacking all three inversions. The associated subclasses
L(G_{\mathrm{p}},\gamma)
for
\gamma=i
,
\theta
, and
i\theta
comprise

G_{\mathrm{p}}
,
G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\gamma}
and all
\gamma
-
minor
groups
G_{\mathrm{p}}\langle\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle_{\gamma}
.

If the subclass root
G_{0}^{\gamma}
still contains another black-white
symmetry, it has to be different from
\gamma
. For the cases
\gamma=i
or
\theta
, the black-white symmetry present in
G_{0}^{\gamma}

will be

\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}i&\mbox{if $\gamma=\theta$}\\[2.84544pt]
\theta&\mbox{if $\gamma=i$}\;.\end{array}\right.

(34)

The case
\gamma=i\theta
requires a more elaborate discussion that is
relegated to Appendix
A.6
. Here we continue with a
brief consideration of the cases
\gamma=i
or
\theta
.

If
G_{0}^{\gamma}
is a
\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
-
major group, then

G_{0}^{\gamma}=G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}}
. For
\gamma=i
or

\theta
, the subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}},\gamma)
contains
all the
\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
-
major groups in class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, as well as the type
-
and
type
-
groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
.

If
G_{0}^{\gamma}
is a
\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}
-
minor group, then

G_{0}^{\gamma}=G_{\mathrm{p}}\langle\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle_{\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}}
. For

\gamma=i
or
\theta
,

L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\langle\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle_{\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}},\gamma)
contains

G_{\mathrm{p}}\langle\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle_{\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}}
,

G_{\mathrm{p}}\langle\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\rangle_{\gamma_{\mathrm{d}}}\times C_{\gamma}
and

G_{\mathrm{p}}\{\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\}
. The
subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}],\theta)

furthermore contains
G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:JJJp}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}]\,(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1^{\prime})}[\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}])
(or the type-
group of the
same form) and
G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:iii-IIIp}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}]\,(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(2)}[\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}])
. Similarly,
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}),i)
contains
G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:JJJp}}}

(or the type-
group of the same form) and

G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:iiip-III}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)})\,[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(2)}(\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}})]
.

The relation between group types and subclasses is summarized in
Table
10
. Groups in a given row in the upper part
of this table belong to the same subclass
L(G_{0}^{i},i)
, with
G_{0}^{i}

being the group in the first column. Similarly, groups in a given column
belong to the same subclass
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
, with
G_{0}^{\theta}

being the group in the first row. The groups of type
and

belong to the same subclasses
L(G_{0}^{i},i)
and

L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
as the group of type
. Similarly,
the groups of type
or
belong to the same
subclasses
L(G_{0}^{i},i)
and
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
as the group of
type
. The composition of subclasses
L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)
is discussed in Appendix
A.6

and summarized in the lower part of Table
10
.

Application of the duality transformation
i\leftrightarrow\theta

to the groups in a subclass
L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)
with
\gamma=i
or
\theta
generates the subclass
L(G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{\gamma},\gamma_{\mathrm{d}})
, with the subclass roots
G_{0}^{\gamma}\;\leftrightarrow\;G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{\gamma}
being dual partners. This corresponds to
mapping rows onto columns and
vice versa
in the upper part
of Table
10
. In contrast, every subclass
L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)\equiv L(G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{i\theta},i\theta)
is mapped onto itself when the duality transformation is
applied to the groups it comprises. Therefore, the subclass
classifications
L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)
do not reflect a triadic
relationship (
31
). More generally, for each pair

\gamma^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime\prime}
of inversion symmetries, one can establish a
pair of
\gamma^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}
-dual subclasses
L(G_{0}^{\gamma^{\prime}},\gamma^{\prime})
and
L(G_{0}^{\gamma^{\prime\prime}},\gamma^{\prime\prime})
, as
well as the
\gamma^{\prime}\leftrightarrow\gamma^{\prime\prime}
-self-dual subclass
L(G_{0}^{\tilde{\gamma}},\tilde{\gamma})
with
\tilde{\gamma}=\gamma^{\prime}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
. In this work, we adhere to the nomenclature
whereby the term duality without a specifier refers to SIS-TIS
duality associated with the exchange
i\leftrightarrow\theta
.

A.6
Form of subclasses
\boldsymbol{L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)}

The concept of subclasses of magnetic point groups is introduced in
Appendix
A.5
. While the form of subclasses
L(G_{0}^{i},i)
and
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
is straightforward, the form of
subclasses
L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)
discussed in the following
is more subtle.

Every magnetic point group
G\in L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
has the general
form
[
56
]

G=\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}+i\{g_{\nu}^{(i)}\}+\theta\{g_{\nu}^{(\theta)}\}+i\theta\{g_{\nu}^{(i\theta)}\}\,\,.

(35)

Here
\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\subseteq G_{\mathrm{p}}
is a proper point
group, and
\{g_{\nu}^{(\gamma)}\}
denotes the set of operations
in the class root
G_{\mathrm{p}}
that appears in
G
in
combination with the black-white symmetry element
\gamma\in\{i,\theta,i\theta\}
. In the following, we assume
G\supsetneq G_{\mathrm{p}}
, i.e., at least one set
\{g_{\nu}^{(\gamma)}\}
is nonempty. The relation
\gamma^{2}=e
and general group
properties of
G
imply the relations

\displaystyle g_{\nu}^{(\gamma)}g_{\mu}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle\in\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}

\displaystyle\mbox{for any fixed $\gamma$}\,\,,

(36a)

\displaystyle g_{\nu}^{(\gamma)}g_{\mu}^{(\gamma^{\prime})}

\displaystyle\in\{g_{\nu^{\prime}}^{(\gamma\gamma^{\prime})}\}

\displaystyle\mbox{for any pair $\gamma\neq\gamma^{\prime}$}\,\,.

(36b)

It follows from Eq. (
36b
) that either only one set
\{g_{\nu}^{(\gamma)}\}
is nonempty, or all three sets are nonempty.

To understand how the mapping
i\theta\mapsto e
transforms the group

G
given in Eq. (
35
), we first note

\theta=i^{2}\,\theta=i\,i\theta\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\theta\mapsto e}}{{\longmapsto}}\;i

(37a)

to find

G\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\theta\mapsto e}}{{\longmapsto}}\;G^{i\theta}_{0}\equiv\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}+\{g_{\nu}^{(i\theta)}\}+i\big(\{g_{\nu}^{(i)}\}+\{g_{\nu}^{(\theta)}\}\big)\,,

(37b)

where clearly
G^{i\theta}_{0}
is again a group.
Alternatively, using

i=i\,\theta^{2}=i\theta\,\theta\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\theta\mapsto e}}{{\longmapsto}}\;\theta

(38a)

yields

G\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i\theta\mapsto e}}{{\longmapsto}}\;G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{i\theta}\equiv\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}+\{g_{\nu}^{(i\theta)}\}+\theta\big(\{g_{\nu}^{(i)}\}+\{g_{\nu}^{(\theta)}\}\big)\,\,.

(38b)

Thus we can identify two equivalent subclass roots
G_{0}^{i\theta}\leftrightarrow G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{i\theta}
that are related via the duality transformation

i\leftrightarrow\theta
, and

L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)\equiv L(G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{i\theta},i\theta)\;.

(39)

Applying the duality transformation
i\leftrightarrow\theta
to
G

in Eq. (
35
) yields the dual partner

G\;\leftrightarrow\;G_{\mathrm{d}}=\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}+\theta\{g_{\nu}^{(i)}\}+i\{g_{\nu}^{(\theta)}\}+i\theta\{g_{\nu}^{(i\theta)}\}\,\,.

(40)

Equations (
37a
) and (
38a
) show that

G_{\mathrm{d}}
maps to the same subclass roots
G^{i\theta}_{0}\leftrightarrow G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{i\theta}
as
G
, i.e., dual
partners
G
and
G_{\mathrm{d}}
belong to the same subclass

L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)\equiv L(G_{0\mathrm{d}}^{i\theta},i\theta)
.

Specializing the composition of a subclass
L(G_{0}^{\gamma},\gamma)

discussed generally in Appendix
A.5
to the case

\gamma=i\theta
, we find that
L(G_{\mathrm{p}},i\theta)
contains
not only
G_{\mathrm{p}}
but also
G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:JJ}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i\theta}
and all the type
-
groups
G_{\mathrm{p}}\{\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}\}\in L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. It follows from Eqs. (
37a
) and
(
38a
) that the subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i},i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\theta},i\theta)
contains the groups of type

,
and
as well as all
type-
and type-
groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. Groups in a subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}],i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i\theta)
contain not only
G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}]
and
G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}})
, but also the type
-

group
G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}})\times C_{i\theta}
. Furthermore,

G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:JJJp}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}]\,(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1^{\prime})}[\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}])
is in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(2)}],i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(2)}),i\theta)
, while

G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:iii-IIIp}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}]\,(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(2)}[\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}])
and
G_{\mathrm{\ref{gcat:iiip-III}}}=G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(2)}]\,(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}[\accentset{\approx}{G}_{\mathrm{p}}])
both belong to

L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}],i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}^{(1)}),i\theta)
.

The combination of
L(G_{\mathrm{p}},i\theta)
with
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i},i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\theta},i\theta)

and all subclasses
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}],i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i\theta)
yields class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
[Eq. (
33
)].

Appendix B
Classification of magnetic point groups

Having developed the classification of magnetic point groups within
a given class, we now apply this classification to the known
continuous and discrete point groups.

B.1
Continuous spherical groups

We have one spherical point group
R\equiv SO(3)
of proper
rotations
C
. By adding
\gamma
-
improper
rotations

\gamma C
with
\gamma=i
,
\theta
or
i\theta
, we obtain the
spherical magnetic groups
G_{\mathrm{sp}}
listed in
Table
11
. These form class
L(R)
. As
R

lacks an index
-
2 subgroup,
L(R)
contains only groups of type

,
,
,
and

. See Table
12
.
We call
R_{i\times\theta}\equiv R\times C_{i\times\theta}
the full rotation group.
All continuous and discrete groups discussed in this work are
subgroups of
R_{i\times\theta}
.

B.2
Continuous axial groups

There exist two proper continuous axial point groups, the cyclic
group
C_{\infty}\equiv SO(2)
and the dihedral group
D_{\infty}
. By
adding SIS and TIS to these groups, one obtains the cyclic magnetic
groups
G_{c\infty}
listed in Table
13
[class

L(C_{\infty})
] and the dihedral magnetic groups
G_{d\infty}

listed in Table
14
[class
L(D_{\infty})
].
We call
D_{\infty}\times C_{i\times\theta}
the full axial rotation group.
See also Refs.
[
180
,
181
,
182
,
183
,
156
]
for related
work on axial point groups.

Like
R
, the group
C_{\infty}
lacks an index
-
2 subgroup, so
the structure of class
L(C_{\infty})
mirrors that of
L(R)
.
See the upper part of Table
15
. In contrast,
group
D_{\infty}
has the index
-
2 subgroup
C_{\infty}
.
Class
L(D_{\infty})
thus has a richer composition that includes
groups of type
,
,

and
, i.e., the types indicated in the middle row of
the scheme shown in the bottom part of
Table
15
.

The continuous axial groups are the maximal groups of many important
physical quantities, see Table
3
.
Therefore, in the following, we review in more detail the IRs of the
continuous axial groups that we refer to repeatedly in this work.
The cyclic groups
G_{c\infty}
are Abelian so that all their IRs
are one-dimensional. Under these groups, the transformation
behavior of tensor components
T_{\ell m}^{ss^{\prime}}
of
R_{i\times\theta}
depends
only on
m
but not on
\ell
; for fixed
m
but different values of

\ell
(with
|m|\leq\ell
), the components
T_{\ell m}^{ss^{\prime}}

transform the same way under groups
G_{c\infty}
. The invariant
tensors of groups
G_{c\infty}
are then the tensor components

T_{\ell 0}^{ss^{\prime}}
(Table
13
). Similar to

T_{00}^{++}
under the spherical groups, tensor components
T_{\ell 0}^{++}
are invariant under all cyclic groups
G_{c\infty}
.

The dihedral groups
G_{d\infty}
are non-Abelian. Under groups

G_{d\infty}
, the tensor components
T_{\ell 0}^{ss^{\prime}}
of
R_{i\times\theta}

transform according to one-dimensional IRs, whereas tensor
components
T_{\ell,\pm m}^{ss^{\prime}}
with
m\neq 0
pairwise transform
according to two-dimensional IRs. More specifically, the
transformation properties depend on the parity of
\ell
. For even

\ell
(“
g
”), tensor components
T_{g0}^{ss^{\prime}}
are even under

\pi
rotations
C_{2}
about an axis perpendicular to the main axis
of the dihedral groups (chosen as the
z
axis in
Table
14
), whereas for odd
\ell
(“
u
”),
tensor components
T_{u0}^{ss^{\prime}}
are odd under rotations
C_{2}

[
179
]
. The invariant tensor components
T_{\ell 0}^{ss^{\prime}}
under the dihedral groups
G_{d\infty}
thus distinguish
between
\ell
even or odd, see Table
14
.
But similar to tensor components
T_{\ell 0}^{ss^{\prime}}
under the cyclic
groups, tensor components with different even or different odd
values of
\ell
behave the same.

The cyclic groups in Table
13
are subgroups of
the dihedral groups in Table
14
. The
maximal groups of tensor components
T_{\ell 0}^{ss^{\prime}}
are thus
generally the dihedral groups that provide a more fine-grained
classification of the symmetries of
T_{\ell 0}^{ss^{\prime}}
based on the
parity of
\ell
, see Table
14
. As noted
above, tensor components
T_{u0}^{ss^{\prime}}
change sign under
\pi

rotations
C_{2}
about an axis perpendicular to the
z
axis

[
179
]
. The transformation behavior of these tensor
components under the full axial group
D_{\infty}\times C_{i\times\theta}
as
listed in Table
14
thus defines
polar
directions
with signature
ss^{\prime}
. For the three polar directions

T_{u0}^{-+}
,
T_{u0}^{+-}
, and
T_{u0}^{--}
the respective
maximal groups are listed in Table
14
.

Interestingly, inspection of Table
14
shows
that an electrotoroidal tensor component
T_{u0}^{++}
is not
invariant under any of the continuous dihedral groups. It is only
invariant under continuous cyclic groups
G_{c\infty}
, including

C_{\infty}\times C_{i\times\theta}
, that do not distinguish between tensor
components
T_{u0}^{++}
and electric tensor components

T_{g0}^{++}
. This fact has the remarkable consequence that, very
generally, one cannot define a setting that would allow one to
distinguish between electrotoroidal tensor components
T_{u0}^{++}

and electric tensor components
T_{g0}^{++}
. Beyond the fact that
tensors
T_{u}^{++}
have no suitable building blocks in
Table
5
, this is the reason why
electrotoroidal tensors
T_{u}^{++}
are not studied further in this
work.

According to the general definition of group types given in
Table
9
, the continuous dihedral groups have
the group types listed in the first column of
Table
14
. A comparison of
Tables
4
and
14
shows
that the range of multipole densities
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
permitted by
these continuous dihedral groups falls short of the range of
densities generally permitted by the categories of polarization
associated with these group types. For example, the group
D_{\infty}\times C_{\theta}
has the type
that belongs to the
electropolar category. However, the group
D_{\infty}\times C_{\theta}
does
not allow odd
-
\ell
electric multipoles (
-+
). This result can
be traced back to the fact that the groups in class

L(D_{\infty})
can also be viewed as limiting groups
n\rightarrow\infty
for the groups in class
L(D_{n})
. The lowest degree

\ell_{\min}^{(e,u)}
of odd
-
\ell
electric multipoles permitted
by groups
D_{n}\times C_{\theta}
grows monotonically with increasing

n
, with
\ell_{\min}^{(e,u)}\rightarrow\infty
for
n\rightarrow\infty
(see Table
21
). This does
not affect our classification of categories of polarized matter as
crystals are characterized by finite crystallographic point groups.

Continuous axial symmetries can be useful to represent the limiting
symmetries of physical systems
[
180
]
. Illustrating these by
simple geometrical shapes has a long history
[
180
,
181
,
183
]
, and such simple objects can also be employed as depictions
of finite chiral systems
[
135
]
. Inspired by these earlier
approaches, we use axially symmetric objects (such as those shown in
Table
16
) to explore the five categories
of chirality, see Figs.
4
,

5
and
6
. To ease
cross-referencing between previous works
[
181
,
156
]

discussing axial quantities and ours, we provide
Table
17
relating respective notations for axial
quantities.

B.3
Discrete groups

There are 11 proper crystallographic point groups:
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
,

D_{2}
,
C_{4}
,
D_{4}
,
C_{3}
,
D_{3}
,
C_{6}
,
D_{6}
,
T
, and
O
,
representing the roots for 11 classes. By adding SIS, one obtains
21 improper (with respect to SIS) point groups
[
11
]
; 11 of
these are
i
-
major groups and 10 are
i
-
minor groups. Jointly,
these groups represent the familiar 32 nonmagnetic crystallographic
point groups (ignoring TIS). By also adding TIS, the 122 magnetic
crystallographic point groups are obtained
[
19
,
21
,
10
,
51
]
. The 32 type
-
II (i.e.,
\theta
-
major) groups
among these are the crystallographic point groups representing
paramagnetic solids. The same groups also characterize
antiferromagnetic crystals with nonsymmorphic space groups
containing symmetry elements that combine nonprimitive translations
with time inversion
[
21
,
119
]
, see Sec
II.9
.
All other magnetic point groups describe crystals with magnetic
order where TIS is broken.

The full set of 122 magnetic crystallographic point groups can be
organized into 11 classes
[
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
]
. These
classes are generalizations of the
Laue
classes that are
usually defined in terms of the improper groups
G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i}

[
66
]
. In a similar spirit, the Laue classes of
magnetic crystallographic point groups have previously been defined
in terms of the paramagnetic groups
G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i\times\theta}

[
59
]
. Here we find it more advantageous to organize these
Laue classes in terms of their respective proper point groups

G_{\mathrm{p}}

[
58
]
. See Table
18
.

The composition of the 11 (Laue) classes of magnetic point groups
derived from the proper crystallographic point groups partly
mirrors, but also extends that of the continuous groups. In
particular, the structure of
L(C_{1})
,
L(C_{3})
and
L(T)
is
entirely analogous to that of
L(R)
and
L(C_{\infty})
(compare
Tables
15
and
18
),
which is due to the fact that, like
R
and
C_{\infty}
, the class
roots
C_{1}
,
C_{3}
and
T
have no index
-
2 subgroup. Similarly,
the structure of
L(C_{2})
,
L(C_{4})
,
L(D_{3})
,
L(C_{6})
and
L(O)

mirrors that of
L(D_{\infty})
, because the respective class roots all
have one index
-
2 subgroup. Class
L(D_{2})
exhibits the basic

L(D_{\infty})
structure, but it contains, in addition, the group

C_{2v}(C_{s})=D_{2}[C_{2}]\,(C_{2}[C_{1}])
which is the only
crystallographic point group of type
.
Classes
L(D_{4})
and
L(D_{6})
exhibit greater complexity because
they have two distinct index
-
2 subgroups, which doubles the
possibilities for constructing groups of type
,

,
,
,

,
and also yields a triad of groups
with respective types
, and
, and

.

Tables
12
,
15
and

18
systematize classes of magnetic point
groups in terms of the SIS-TIS-symmetric group types summarized in
Table
9
. The placement of individual groups
within rows and columns as per the schemes given at the bottom of
each table illustrates SIS-TIS duality, see
Appendix
A.3
. In particular, for each class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, the
main block
has the groups of type

,
,
and

at its four corners. Within this block, the duality transformation
based on the exchange
i\leftrightarrow\theta
maps rows on
columns and vice versa. Groups placed on the diagonal of the main
block are self-dual, and so are the type
,

,
, and
groups
positioned outside of the main block. Using SIS-TIS duality of
magnetic point groups within a class as the organizing principle
differentiates the arrangement of Table
18

from the previously developed periodic arrangement of the 122
crystallographic magnetic point groups shown, e.g., in Table 2 of
Ref.
[
59
]
.

The organizing principle of Tables
12
,

15
and
18
follows that
shown for a general class in the upper part of Table
10
.
Thus, in all these tables, groups within the main block of a class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})

[
184
]
are placed such that groups
in a given row belong to the same subclass
L(G_{0}^{i},i)
, where
G_{0}^{i}

is the group in the first column. Groups in a given column of the main
block belong to the same subclass
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
,
G_{0}^{\theta}

being the group in the first row. See Appendices
A.5

and
A.6
for a detailed discussion of subclass
structures.

Subgroup relations and isomorphisms between groups in a given class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
follow Fig.
10
. See also explicit
tabulations of subgroup relations for the ordinary crystallographic
point groups
[
16
,
185
]
and for the magnetic
groups
[
186
,
187
]
. Table
9
lists
the number of point groups of each type among the 122
crystallographic magnetic point groups. The triadic structure
underlies the appearance of certain
magic numbers

[
55
,
56
,
62
]
when groups are collated based on
particular physical characteristics described by tensor quantities.
See Appendix
E
for a detailed discussion of this
point.

Table
19
lists the lowest rank

\ell_{\min}
of nontrivial multipole densities permitted by
all 122 crystallographic magnetic point groups.
