C.1
General theory

To illustrate how compound tensors can be formed from electric and
magnetic tensors, Table
20
gives the multiplication
table for electric and magnetic IRs
D_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
of the full
rotation group
R_{i\times\theta}
with
\ell\leq 3
. The range of ranks
\ell

contained in a product representation is given by Eq. (
3
).

For example, Table
20
shows that a magnetic monopole

T_{0}^{--}
can arise as a product of an electric and a magnetic
multipole with the same
\ell>0
. These multipoles with
\ell>0
break spherical symmetry so that the symmetry group of a system
realizing such a synthetic magnetic monopole cannot be the spherical
magnetic group
R_{i\theta}
(that would be the symmetry group of
an elementary magnetic monopole if it existed). However, a product of
electric and magnetic multipoles with large
\ell
can be
arbitrarily close to spherical symmetry, which indicates an avenue
for realizing “realistic” synthetic magnetic
monopoles
[
188
,
189
]
.

Products (
4
) of electric and magnetic tensors
yield not only electric and magnetic compound tensors but also
electrotoroidal and magnetotoroidal compound tensors (marked
with bold subscripts
\ell
in Table
20
). For
example, an electric quadrupole
T_{2}^{++}
and an electric dipole

T_{1}^{-+}
jointly give rise to an electrotoroidal quadrupole

T_{2}^{-+}
.

Note that an electrotoroidal compound tensor
T_{1}^{++}
does not
appear in Table
20
. Electrotoroidal compound tensors

T_{\ell}^{++}
with odd
\ell
are realized by antisymmetric
(noncommuting) products
(T_{\ell_{1}}^{ss^{\prime}}\times T_{\ell_{1}}^{ss^{\prime}})_{\mathrm{a}}
of the same tensor
T_{\ell_{1}}^{ss^{\prime}}

or by products
T_{\ell_{1}}^{ss^{\prime}}\times\tilde{T}_{\ell_{2}}^{ss^{\prime}}
of
two distinct tensors
T_{\ell_{1}}^{ss^{\prime}}
and

\tilde{T}_{\ell_{2}}^{ss^{\prime}}
with the same signature
ss^{\prime}

[
67
]
. However, odd
-
\ell
IRs
D_{\ell}^{++}
appearing
off-diagonally in the multiplication table have
\ell\geq 3
. The
IR
D_{1}^{++}
arises only on the diagonal. Assuming that for each

\ell
a system has only one electric and one magnetic multipole

T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
, second-order products of these multipoles thus
cannot realize an electrotoroidal compound tensor
T_{1}^{++}
. This
is in line with the difficulties encountered in
Table
5
and Appendix
B.2

to realize and interpret such tensors.

Toroidal scalars
T_{0}^{\mp\pm}
likewise do not arise as
second-order products of electric and magnetic tensors, see
Table
20
, but they do arise in third-order products
of electric and magnetic tensors, for example:

\left.\mbox{\begin{tabular}[]{nLn}\raisebox{0.0pt}[0.0pt]{$\left.\mbox{\begin{tabular}[]{nLn}T_{2}^{++}\\[4.30554pt]
T_{4}^{++}\end{tabular}}\right\}$}\;T_{3}^{++}\\[17.22217pt]
T_{3}^{\mp\pm}\end{tabular}}\right\}\;T_{0}^{\mp\pm}.

(41)

Similar to magnetic scalars
T_{0}^{--}
, the toroidal scalars

T_{0}^{-+}
and
T_{0}^{+-}
can only arise in electromagnetic media
that break spherical symmetry.

Inspection of Table
20
and Eq. (
41
) shows that all nontrivial compound tensors
(except
T_{1}^{++}

[
89
]
) can be obtained as products
combining one nontrivial and one or two trivial tensors. By
definition, trivial tensors have the signature
++
. Therefore,
toroidal compound tensors inherit the signature of the nontrivial
electromagnetic tensor they are composed of, while the index
\ell^{\prime}

of toroidal compound tensors has the opposite parity as the index

\ell
of the nontrivial tensor they are composed of. Consistent
with Tables
5

and
6
, we thus interpret toroidal
compound tensors as manifestations of the nontrivial electromagnetic
tensor they are composed of.

The above group-theoretical analysis of compound tensors is
independent of specific realizations of the tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}

as discussed in Table
5
. It is equally
applicable to finite and infinite periodic systems.

Consistent with our definition of categories of multipole order
based on the signature
ss^{\prime}
, the terms electropolarization
(signature
-+
), magnetopolarization (
+-
), and
antimagnetopolarization (
--
) refer jointly to the respective
odd
-
\ell
electric, odd
-
\ell
magnetic, and even
-
\ell

magnetic multipoles, as well as the corresponding toroidal compound
moments with the same signature, unless we want to emphasize the
composition of toroidal compound tensors.

C.2
Examples of compound moments

Reference
[
18
]
contains a detailed study of how electric and
magnetic multipoles manifest themselves via characteristic terms in
the electronic band structure of a crystal, using variations of
lonsdaleite and diamond as examples. A careful inspection shows
that a number of examples discussed in that work rely on compound
moments.

For instance, we can have three qualitatively distinct homogeneous
polynomials in
k,\sigma
of degree 2 in a crystal, two of which are
compound moments. (i) Crystal structures with point groups
T
and

O
permit the crystallographic tensor
{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
. This
term transforms like an electrotoroidal compound scalar
T_{0}^{-+}

that arises as a product (
41
) of a nontrivial
odd
-
\ell
electric multipole and two trivial even
-
\ell

electric multipoles. See also Sec.
III
. (ii)
Electropolar crystal structures such as wurtzite (point group

C_{6v}
, ignoring TIS) permit an electric dipole density that
manifests itself via the Rashba term
k_{x}\sigma_{y}-k_{y}\sigma_{x}

[
102
]
. Under
R_{i\times\theta}
, this term transforms like the
component
T_{10}^{-+}
of an electric dipole density. (iii) The
cubic zincblende structure (point group
T_{d}
) permits an electric
octupole density (
\ell=3
) that manifests itself via the
Dresselhaus term
\sigma_{x}k_{x}(k_{y}^{2}-k_{z}^{2})+\mbox{cp}
, where
cp

denotes cyclic permutation
[
103
]
. Under
R_{i\times\theta}
, this term
transforms like the component
T_{3,-2}^{-+}+T_{3,2}^{-+}
of an
electric octupole density (
\ell=3
). When uniaxial strain is
applied to a zincblende structure (in [001] direction, thus reducing
the crystal symmetry from
T_{d}
to
D_{2d}
), we obtain the crystallographic
tensor
k_{x}\sigma_{x}-k_{y}\sigma_{y}
. Under
R_{i\times\theta}
, this term
transforms like the component
T_{2,-2}^{-+}+T_{2,2}^{-+}
of an
electrotoroidal quadrupole density that arises as a product
(
4
) of the nontrivial electric octupole density
(
\ell=3
) that exists already in unstrained zincblende and a
trivial strain-induced electric quadrupole density (
\ell=2
).
The electrotoroidal quadrupole density represents the lowest
even-rank electrotoroidal moment density permitted in a structure
with point group
D_{2d}
(Appendix
D
,
Table
21
).

Pristine lonsdaleite has four identical atoms per unit cell; its
crystallographic point group is
D_{6h}=D_{6}\times C_{i}
(ignoring
TIS). If the four sublattices are occupied by two types of atoms as
in Fig. 2(b) of Ref.
[
18
]
, this results in an electric
octupolarization (
\ell=3
), and the point-group symmetry is
reduced to
D_{3h}
. In addition, two new invariants arise in
the band structure (to lowest order in
{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}
)
[
18
]

\displaystyle H_{1}

\displaystyle=c_{1}\,\sigma_{z}\,k_{y}\left(3k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2}\right)

(42a)

\displaystyle H_{2}

\displaystyle=c_{2}\,k_{z}\left[\sigma_{x}k_{x}k_{y}+{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\sigma_{y}(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})\right]\,,

(42b)

where
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are material-specific prefactors. These terms can be
rearranged as

\displaystyle H^{(3)}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{5}{8}}(H_{1}-2H_{2})

(43a)

\displaystyle H^{(4)}

\displaystyle={\textstyle\frac{3}{8}}(H_{1}+6H_{2})\,.

(43b)

Here, the first term transforms like the component
T_{3,-3}^{-+}+T_{3,3}^{-+}
of an electric octupole density (
\ell=3
), while
the second term transforms like the component
T_{4,-3}^{-+}+T_{4,3}^{-+}
of an electrotoroidal hexadecapole density (
\ell=4
). The latter represents the lowest even-rank electrotoroidal
moment density permitted in a structure with point group

D_{3h}
(Appendix
D
,
Table
21
). It is realized as a compound
moment density that combines the nontrivial electric
octupole density (
\ell=3
) with the trivial electric quadrupole
density (
\ell=2
) present already in pristine lonsdaleite.

An important example of compound moments arises in the context of
antimagnetopolarizations. Very generally, unlike
electropolarizations and magnetopolarizations, even
-
\ell

antimagnetopolarizations
T_{\ell}^{--}
have no indicators in the
band structure associated with them, see
Table
5
.
However, antimagnetopolarizations combined with trivial
even
-
\ell
parapolarizations give rise to odd
-
\ell

magnetotoroidal densities
T_{\ell}^{--}
that manifest themselves in
the band structure via terms proportional to odd powers in
k

(Table
5
). Therefore, we associate
antimagnetopolarizations with such terms
k^{2n+1}
. While
odd
-
\ell
electropolarizations and odd
-
\ell

magnetopolarizations permit a simple correspondence between the rank

\ell
and a minimal-degree polynomial representation of the
associated tensors (Table
5
), no such
correspondence exists for even
-
\ell
antimagnetopolarizations.

For example, in a diamond crystal structure, a magnetic quadrupole
density (
\ell=2
) gives rise to a term
k_{z}(k_{x}^{2}-k_{y}^{2})

[
106
,
18
]
. Under
R_{i\times\theta}
, this term transforms
like the component
T_{3,-2}^{--}+T_{3,2}^{--}
of a
magnetotoroidal octupole density (
\ell=3
). This term arises as
a product (
4
) of a nontrivial magnetic quadrupole
density (
\ell=2
) and a trivial electric hexadecapole density
(
\ell=4
) present already in pristine diamond.

A complete review of the examples in Ref.
[
18
]
is beyond the
scope of the present work. Tables
21
and

22
in Appendix
D
give the
lowest ranks of electromagnetic multipoles and toroidal compound
multipoles in crystal structures with different crystallographic
point groups
G
.

Appendix D
Spherical tensors and multipole order permitted by the 122
crystallographic magnetic point groups

This appendix provides explicit details about which spherical
tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
are permitted by the 122 crystallographic
magnetic point groups. The information given here is directly
relevant for discussing electric, magnetic and toroidal multipole
order in crystals, see Sec.
II.2
. As
discussed below, the information is furthermore relevant for
materials and property tensors that are commonly represented by
Cartesian rank
-
N
tensors.

Table
21
is devoted to
\theta
-
even tensors

T_{\ell}^{s+}
. It lists the number of independent components such
tensors with rank
\ell\leq 3
have under the nonmagnetic
(type
-
II) groups belonging to the 11 Laue classes
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
.
For comparison, the same information is also provided for type
-
II
groups in the three classes of continuous cyclic, dihedral and
spherical point groups. To streamline the presentation, the
universally allowed electric monopolarization
T_{0}^{++}
has been
omitted. Basic physical properties can be read off from this table.
For example, groups allowing
T_{0}^{-+}
represent electrochiral
systems, see Sec.
III.1.2
, and groups allowing

T_{1}^{-+}
represent pyroelectric and ferroelectric
systems
[
13
]
.

Table
21
together with
Table
18
can be used to infer the properties
of tensors
T_{\ell}^{s+}
in all 122 magnetic point groups.
Specifically, Table
21
gives the properties of
tensors for the crystallographic point groups
G_{0}^{\theta}\times C_{\theta}
that appear in Table
18
in the last
row of the main block
[
184
]
for a class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. The structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{s+}
allowed
under
G_{0}^{\theta}\times C_{\theta}
is repeated for all members of the
subclass
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)\subset L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, which
are the groups positioned in the column headed by
G_{0}^{\theta}

within the same class’s main block. The formal basis for these
congruences is elaborated in detail in Appendix
E
.

Table
21
also tabulates the lowest even
(“
g
”) and odd (“
u
”) orders
\ell
of electric (“e”) and
electrotoroidal (“et”) multipole densities permitted in each
crystal class. The electric monopolarization
T_{0}^{++}
is
trivially allowed under all point groups and, therefore, is not
considered for determining
\ell_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\mathrm{e,g})}
.
Instead, to convey information that is specific to each group, we
tabulate the lowest
nonzero
even order of
parapolarizations allowed under each group.

Table 22:
Number of independent components
of
\theta
-
odd spherical tensors
T_{\ell}^{s-}
permitted by
type
-
III magnetic point groups (considering ranks
\ell\leq 3
).
Tensors
T_{\ell}^{+-}
represent magnetopolarizations and

T_{\ell}^{--}
are antimagnetopolarizations. A “0” indicates
that the corresponding tensor is forbidden by symmetry. The
magnetic monopolarization
T_{0}^{+-}
has been omitted, as it is
universally forbidden under type
-
III groups. The quantities

\ell_{\mathrm{min}}^{(\mathrm{m/mt,g/u})}
indicate, respectively,
for each group, the lowest symmetry-allowed even (“
g
”) and odd
(“
u
”) orders
\ell
of magnetic (“m”) and magnetotoroidal
(“mt”) multipole densities
\ell>0
, i.e., ignoring
monopolarizations. Both the Schönflies and Hermann-Mauguin
notations
[
190
]
are given for each group, as well as the
group’s type according to the classification in
Table
9
.

Table
22
pertains to
\theta
-
odd tensors
T_{\ell}^{s-}
.
It lists the number of independent components for such tensors with
rank
\ell\leq 3
under the type
-
III magnetic point groups (58
crystallographic, 7 continuous). According to the classification
introduced in Appendix
A.3
, these comprise the groups
of type
,
,
,
,

,
,
,
and

. In the other types,
\theta
-
odd tensors are either
equivalent to the
\theta
-
even ones listed in Table
21

(types
,
and
), or they are forbidden
by symmetry (types
,
and
).
More generally, the entries in Table
22
are related to
those given in Table
21
as per the
relationships illustrated by small
2\times 2
matrices in
Tables
12
,
15
, and

18
, and further elucidated in
Appendix
E
. As the magnetotoroidal monopolarization

T_{0}^{+-}
is forbidden by symmetry under all type
-
III groups, it
has been omitted to simplify the presentation.
Table
22
also lists the lowest even- and
odd
-
\ell
magnetic and magnetotoroidal multipole densities
with
\ell>0
permitted under a given type
-
III magnetic point group.

A variety of magnetism-related physical properties can be inferred
from Table
22
. For example, ferromagnetism occurs
for groups allowing the tensor
T_{1}^{+-}

[
20
,
21
]
. In
groups where
T_{1}^{+-}
is forbidden, the presence of
T_{\ell}^{+-}

with odd
\ell\geq 3
signals altermagnetism
[
82
,
18
]
.
Axion electrodynamics can be associated with a finite

T_{0}^{--}

[
191
]
.

Appendix E
Rationalizing symmetry-allowed tensor components for
magnetic point groups

This appendix discusses commonalities regarding the shape of tensors
describing systems belonging to the same Laue class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
.
Combining rotations in a proper point group
G_{\mathrm{p}}
with space
inversion
i
and time inversion
\theta
yields the magnetic point
groups of class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. The relations between
symmetry transformations for these groups manifest in commonalities
regarding the form of symmetry-allowed tensors. Similarities in the
form of crystal-property matrices are apparent from their
tabulations (see, e.g., Refs.
[
13
,
59
,
96
]
and
Appendix
D
above), and they also underpin the
observation of Opechowski’s magic relations
[
58
]
.

We visualize the commonalities between tensor configurations for
groups within a class via small
2\times 2
matrices such as

(\raisebox{0.86108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\begin{array}[]{@{}>{\scriptstyle}cs{0.08em}>{\scriptstyle}c@{}}\mcirc\hfil\hskip 0.65556pt&\mfilledstar\\[-6.88889pt]
\mdiamond\hfil\hskip 0.65556pt&\mdiamond\end{array}$})
shown in Tables
9
,

10
,
12
,

15
, and
18
. Symbols in
these matrices mark congruences between tensors with signature
++

(upper left),
+-
(upper right),
-+
(lower left) and
--
(lower
right) for a given group and between different groups within a given
class. Filled symbols indicate that a rank
-
1 tensor is allowed
(compare Tables
21

and
22
), i.e., filled symbols reflect the fact
that a group possesses a polar direction as discussed in
Sec.
II.6
. A missing symbol indicates that tensors
with the respective signature are forbidden for any
\ell
. For
example, the matrix
(\raisebox{0.86108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\begin{array}[]{@{}>{\scriptstyle}cs{0.08em}>{\scriptstyle}c@{}}\mcirc\hfil\hskip 0.65556pt&\mfilledstar\\[-6.88889pt]
\mdiamond\hfil\hskip 0.65556pt&\mdiamond\end{array}$})
listed in
Table
18
for the group
D_{2d}(S_{4})=D_{4}[D_{2}]\,(C_{4}[C_{2}])
[class
L(D_{4})
] indicates that this group
allows tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
for all four signatures
ss^{\prime}
. In
particular, this group allows rank
-
1 tensors with signature
+-
,
and rank
-
\ell
tensors with signature
-+
have the same
structure as rank
-
\ell
tensors with signature
--
(for
\ell\geq\ell_{\mathrm{min}}
as listed in Tables
21

and
22
). Finally, repeated appearances of the
symbol
\mcirc
in the matrices listed for other groups in class

L(D_{4})
indicate that the respective tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}

allowed for these groups have the same structure as the tensors

T_{\ell}^{++}
allowed for the group
D_{2d}(S_{4})
[and similarly
for the other symbols in
(\raisebox{0.86108pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{$\begin{array}[]{@{}>{\scriptstyle}cs{0.08em}>{\scriptstyle}c@{}}\mcirc\hfil\hskip 0.65556pt&\mfilledstar\\[-6.88889pt]
\mdiamond\hfil\hskip 0.65556pt&\mdiamond\end{array}$})
].
These congruences of tensor configurations for groups within a given
class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
imply that knowing the structure of rank
-
\ell

tensors for nonmagnetic groups of type
and

(as discussed, e.g., by Nye
[
13
]
) is
sufficient to infer the structure of rank
-
\ell
tensors for all
groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
.

In the following, we use the concepts of duality (
28
)
and triadic relationships (
31
) between magnetic point
groups to formally derive, for each signature
ss^{\prime}
, the congruences
of tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}
among the groups in a given class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. In particular, we show how it is possible to relate the
structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{s-}
allowed for magnetic groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
to the structure of the tensors
T_{\ell}^{s+}
allowed for
nonmagnetic groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
. While our discussion focuses on
spherical tensors
T_{\ell}^{ss^{\prime}}

[
67
,
68
]
that are
relevant, e.g., for describing multipole order in solids (see
discussion in Sec.
II
), the congruences also apply
to Cartesian tensors representing materials properties
[
13
,
14
,
56
,
57
,
59
,
61
,
60
,
58
]
, see
Appendix
D
.

E.1
Tensors
\boldsymbol{T_{\ell}^{++}}
: Parapolarizations

Parapolarizations, having signature
++
, are agnostic with respect
to both SIS and TIS; hence, their structure is determined by symmetry
under proper rotations only. Tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}
thus have the
same structure for all groups in a given class
L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, and
this structure depends only on the symmetries present in the class
root
G_{\mathrm{p}}
.

E.2
Tensors
\boldsymbol{T_{\ell}^{-+}}
: Electropolarizations

Tensors that are odd under SIS and even under TIS are unaffected by
whether TIS may be broken. Tensors
T_{\ell}^{-+}
thus have
the same structure for all groups in a subclass
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)\subset L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, coinciding with the structure of

T_{\ell}^{-+}
for the subclass root
G_{0}^{\theta}
. For the groups in
the subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}},\theta)
, the structure of tensors

T_{\ell}^{-+}
is the same as the structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}

because these tensors have the same structure for the subclass root

G_{\mathrm{p}}
.

In contrast, the structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{-+}
for the groups
in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}],\theta)
is, in
general, different from the structure of the tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}
.
No tensors of the form
T_{\ell}^{-+}
are allowed for any groups in
a subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i},\theta)
.
Table
10
illustrates the congruence of tensors

T_{\ell}^{-+}
between groups in a given subclass
L(G_{0}^{\theta},\theta)
(corresponding to columns in the upper part of the table).

E.3
Tensors
\boldsymbol{T_{\ell}^{+-}}
: Magnetopolarizations

Tensors with signature
+-
are unaffected by whether SIS may be
broken. For all groups in a subclass
L(G_{0}^{i},i)\subset L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
(corresponding to rows in the upper part of
Table
10
) tensors
T_{\ell}^{+-}
thus have the same
structure, coinciding with the structure of
T_{\ell}^{+-}
for the
subclass root
G_{0}^{i}
. In particular, for the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}},i)
tensors
T_{\ell}^{+-}
have the same structure as
tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}
, while tensors of the form
T_{\ell}^{+-}
are
forbidden by symmetry for all groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\theta},i)
.

For the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i)
, the
structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{+-}
is, in general, different from
that of the tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}
. However, by virtue of the
SIS-TIS duality relation under exchange
i\leftrightarrow\theta
,
the structure of magnetopolarizations
T_{\ell}^{+-}
permitted by the
groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i)
coincides
with the structure of electropolarizations
T_{\ell}^{-+}
permitted
by the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}],\theta)
.

E.4
Tensors
\boldsymbol{T_{\ell}^{--}}
: Antimagnetopolarizations

While tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}
are odd under
i
and
\theta
applied
individually, these tensors are even under the combination

i\theta
. For all groups in a Laue subclass
L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)\subset L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}
thus have
the same structure, coinciding with the structure of
T_{\ell}^{--}

for the subclass root
G_{0}^{i\theta}
. For the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}},i\theta)
, the structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}
is
the same as the structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}
, and such tensors
are forbidden for all groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{\theta},i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}\times C_{i},i\theta)
.

For the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i\theta)\equiv L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}],i\theta)
, the
structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}
is, in general, different from
the structure of the tensors
T_{\ell}^{++}
. However, for the
type
-
group
G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}})
,
tensors
T_{\ell}^{+-}
and
T_{\ell}^{--}
have the same structure.
This group is in the subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i\theta)
. We thus find that for the
groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i\theta)
the
structure of tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}
is the same as the structure of
tensors
T_{\ell}^{+-}
for the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i)
and the structure of
tensors
T_{\ell}^{-+}
for the groups in
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}[\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}],\theta)
. See the bottom part of
Table
10
for the composition of subclasses
L(G_{0}^{i\theta},i\theta)
.

E.5
Inferences facilitated by triadic relationships

By itself, the concept of SIS-TIS duality is insufficient to infer
congruences of tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}
for different groups in a class

L(G_{\mathrm{p}})
, as the signature
--
is mapped onto itself
under the duality transformation
i\leftrightarrow\theta
.
Nevertheless, in the preceding subsection, we leveraged the identical
properties of
T_{\ell}^{+-}
and
T_{\ell}^{--}
tensors under
type
-
groups to predict the form of the tensors
for all groups in the subclass
L(G_{\mathrm{p}}(\tilde{G}_{\mathrm{p}}),i\theta)
. Here we utilize the notion of
triadic relationships discussed in Appendix
A.4
to reveal
further close links between the properties of tensors
T_{\ell}^{--}

and tensors with mixed signatures
+-
and
-+
.

A triad of magnetic point groups consists of a dual pair

G_{\theta}\;\leftrightarrow\;G_{i}
augmented by a self-dual group
G_{i\theta}
.
The diagram (
31
) illustrates our notation, and
Table
9
displays the general form of
magnetic point groups forming a triad. For triadic partners, the
structure of tensors with signature
-+
,
+-
and
--
is
permuted. As a simple nontrivial example, consider the triad
formed by groups of type
,
and

within a class, according to their general forms
given in Table
9
. Here the form of tensors

T_{\ell}^{--}
permitted by the type
-
member

G_{i\theta}
of this triad mirrors the form of the tensors

T_{\ell}^{+-}
allowed under the group
G_{i}
of type

, and the tensors
T_{\ell}^{-+}
associated with

G_{i}
’s dual partner
G_{\theta}
of type
. Thus,
the triads embody a close interrelation of tensors with signatures

-+
,
+-
and
--
for magnetic point groups within a given class.

Appendix F
Chiral materials candidates

This appendix tabulates examples of materials candidates
realizing the categories of chirality introduced in
Sec.
III.1
.

F.1
Electrochiral materials

The physical property of electropolar electrochiral materials that
is currently attracting the most interest is the expected radial
spin texture
{\bm{\mathrm{\sigma}}}\cdot{\bm{\mathrm{k}}}

[
161
]
and the
associated phenomenon of a current-induced
magnetization
[
154
,
192
]
. Typical illustrations of the
chirality-dependent spin texture look like
Fig.
7
(d). The crucial importance of
crystallographic symmetries for shaping spin textures arising from
electrochirality has only recently been recognized
[
162
,
193
]
.
Insights from Ref.
[
162
]
and our more detailed discussion
above are useful for interpreting and guiding precision measurements
such as those performed recently for the paradigmatic chiral metal
Te
[
194
,
195
]
. More electropolar electrochiral materials
candidates are shown in Table
23(a)
. Selected
examples for symmetry-appropriate spin textures are shown in
Figs.
7
and
9
. See also
Ref.
[
162
]
.

As opposed to the nonmagnetic electropolar electrochiral materials,
multipolar electrochiral materials show either ferromagnetism or
antiferromagnetism. The topological properties of the chiral
antiferromagnets have been discussed in Refs.
[
196
,
197
]
.
Materials candidates for this type are also listed in
Table
23(a)
.

F.2
Magnetochiral materials

The presence of a magnetotoroidal scalar
T_{0}^{+-}
is a sufficient
condition for magnetochirality. Some physical consequences of having

T_{0}^{+-}
are discussed in Ref.
[
142
]
, focusing on the
effect of terms coupling
T_{0}^{+-}
to scalar products of dipoles
transforming like
T_{0}^{+-}
(i.e.,
T_{1}^{ss^{\prime}}\cdot T_{1}^{s(-s^{\prime})}
).

A more explicit electronic-structure implication of magnetochirality is
altermagnetism in materials candidates where ferromagnetism is
forbidden (“inadmissible” magnetochiral antiferromagnets). Candidates
for magnetochiral materials (both magnetopolar and multipolar types)
are listed in Table
23(b)
. A recently investigated
magnetochiral materials example is Co
2
SiO
4

[
157
]
.

F.3
Antimagnetochiral materials

Prominently discussed materials examples for this category are
antimagnetopolar LiMnPO
4

[
39
]
and multipolar
LuFeO
3

[
198
]
. Interest in these compounds has been fueled
by their realization of a monopolization
T_{0}^{--}
whose value has
been estimated using computational-chemistry
approaches
[
39
,
198
]
. The main physical implication is the
isotropic linear magnetoelectric response (“axion
electrodynamics”
[
191
,
199
]
). Candidates for
antimagnetochiral materials (both antimagnetopolar and multipolar
types) are listed in Table
23(c)
.

F.4
Multichiral materials

Candidates for multichiral materials are listed in
Table
23(d)
. These are necessarily multipolar
(see Table
9
). The existence of four
enantiomorphs is a distinctive property of such materials.
A recently investigated multichiral materials example is
BaCoSiO
4

[
146
]
.

[

of Ref.
[

. The electrochiral magnetic-point-group

classification

.
